 BE IT ENACTED BY THE COUNCIL OF THE DISTRICT OF COLUMBIA, That this 108 
act may be cited as the “Fiscal Year 2026 Budget Support Act of 2025”. 109 
TITLE I. GOVERNMENT DIRECTION AND SUPPORT 110 
SUBTITLE A. LIMITATIONS ON LIABILITY AGAINST THE DISTRICT 111 
 Sec. 1001. Short title. 112 
 This subtitle may be cited as the “Limitations on Liability Against the District of 113 
Columbia Act of 2025”. 114 
 Sec. 1002. Limitations on liability for unliquidated damage claims against the District. 115 
 (a) The liability of the District of Columbia, including its instrumentalities, for 116 
unliquidated damages to persons or property arising out of the same incident or occurrence shall 117 
not exceed $500,000 regardless of the number of claimants or beneficiaries who share in the 118 
award; except, that the liability of the District of Columbia for unliquidated damages to persons 119 
or property arising from intentional wrongful acts or omissions shall not exceed $1 million for all 120 
claims arising out of the same incident or occurrence, regardless of the number of claimants or 121 
beneficiaries who share in the award. 122

6 
 (b) No portion of an award against the District for unliquidated damages to a person may 123 
include noneconomic damages unless a claimant first establishes that the claimant incurred 124 
reasonable and necessary medical expenses of more than $10,000 due to the act or omission of 125 
the District of Columbia for which the claim is made, except in the case of an injury resulting in 126 
permanent disfigurement or death or resulting from an intentional wrongful act. 127 
 (c) This section shall apply to all claims or causes of action against the District of 128 
Columbia, including its instrumentalities, filed in any court or other tribunal of competent 129 
jurisdiction on or after October 1, 2025. 130 
 Sec. 1003. Limited application of the collateral source rule to actions against the District. 131 
 In addition to any other limitation under law, in any action against the District of 132 
Columbia, including its instrumentalities, to recover damages for personal injury, survivorship, 133 
or wrongful death, where the plaintiff seeks to recover for the cost of medical care, dental care, 134 
custodial care, or rehabilitation services, only evidence of actual amounts paid or required to be 135 
paid by or on behalf of the plaintiff shall be admissible for consideration by a judge or jury in 136 
determining damages. 137 
 Sec. 1004. The District of Columbia Employee Non-Liability Act, effective July 14, 1960 138 
(Pub. L. 86-654, 74 Stat. 519; D.C. Official Code § 2-411 et seq.), is amended as follows: 139 
 (a) Section 2 (D.C. Official Code § 2-411) is amended by adding a new paragraph (6A) to 140 
read as follows: 141 
  “(6A) “Gross negligence” means such an extreme deviation from the ordinary 142 
standard of care so as to support a finding of wanton, willful, and reckless disregard or conscious 143 
indifference for the rights and safety of others.  In assessing whether there is such an extreme 144

7 
deviation from the ordinary standard of care, due deference shall be given to the mission giving 145 
rise to the emergency run and the need to respond expeditiously under the circumstances.” 146 
 (b) Section 3 (D.C. Official Code § 2-412) is amended as follows: 147 
  (1) The existing text is designated as subsection (a). 148 
  (2) A new subsection (b) is added to read as follows: 149 
 “(b) In any suit at law that arises out of the operation of a District of Columbia 150 
emergency vehicle on an emergency run, a party’s contributory negligence shall be a complete 151 
bar to recovery by the party. To establish contributory negligence, the District shall not be 152 
required to prove that the party was grossly negligent, and the party’s failure to yield to a vehicle 153 
on an emergency run that has its lights and sirens activated shall constitute contributory 154 
negligence as a matter of law.”. 155 
SUBTITLE B. COMMUNITY AFFAIRS 156 
 Sec. 1011. Short title.    157 
 This subtitle may be cited as the “Community Affairs Amendment Act of 2025”. 158 
 Sec. 1012. Cultural and community affairs grants. 159 
 (a)(1) The Mayor may issue grants to individuals and organizations that provide services 160 
to District residents to support cultural affairs, community relations, and community 161 
partnerships. 162 
  (2) Grants issued under this subsection shall be administered pursuant to the 163 
requirements set forth in the Grant Administration Act of 2013, effective December 24, 2013 164 
(D.C. Law 20-61; D.C. Official Code § 1-328.11 et seq.). 165 
 (b) The Mayor shall provide centralized review and approval of grants issued pursuant to:  166

8 
  (1) Section 3(10) of the Office and Commission on African Affairs Act of 2006, 167 
effective June 8, 2006 (D.C Law 16-111; D.C. Official Code § 2-1392(10)); 168 
  (2) Section 3(b)(3) of the Office on African American Affairs Establishment Act 169 
of 2017, effectively February 17, 2018 (D.C. Law 22-59; D.C. Official Code § 2-1398.02(b)(3)); 170 
  (3) Section 304(c)(9) of the Office on Asian and Pacific Islander Affairs 171 
Establishment Act of 2001, effective October 3, 2001 (D.C. Law 14-28; D.C. Official Code § 2-172 
1373(c)(9)); 173 
  (4) Section 3(3) of the Office on Caribbean Community Affairs Establishment 174 
Act of 2020, effective May 6, 2020 (D.C. Law 23-87; D.C Official Code § 3-1452(3)); 175 
  (5) Section 303(10) of the District of Columbia Latino Community Development 176 
Act, effective September 29, 1976 (D.C. Law 1-86; D.C. Code § 2-1313(10)); 177 
  (6) Sections 4(b)(11), (11A), and (12) and 4a of the Office of Gay, Lesbian, 178 
Bisexual, and Transgender Affairs Act of 2006, effective April 4, 2006 (D.C. Law 16-89; D.C. 179 
Official Code §§ 2-1383(b)(11), (11A), and (12), and 2-1384); 180 
  (7) Section 3(b)(2)(L) of the Office on Ex-Offender Affairs and Commission on 181 
Re-entry and Ex-Offender Affairs Establishment Act of 2006, effective March 8, 2007 (D.C. 182 
Law 16-243; D.C. Official Code § 24-1302(b)(2)(L)); and 183 
  (8) Section 704(6A) of the Office of Veterans Affairs Establishment Act of 2001, 184 
effective October 3, 2001 (D.C. Law 14-28; D.C. Official Code § 49-1003(6A)). 185 
 Sec. 1013. The Commission on Fathers, Men, and Boys Establishment Act of 2014, 186 
effective February 26, 2015 (D.C. Law 20-155; D.C. Official Code § 1072 et seq.), is repealed.   187

 Sec. 1021. Short title. 197 
 This subtitle may be cited as the “Surplus Property Sales Fund Amendment Act of 2025”. 198 
 Sec. 1022. Section 805 of the Procurement Practices Reform Act of 2010, effective 199 
October 22, 2015 (D.C. Law 21-36; D.C. Official Code § 2-358.05), is amended as follows: 200 
 (a) Subsection (c) is amended to read as follows: 201

 Sec. 1031. Short title. 209

10 
 This subtitle may be cited as the “Office of the Chief Technology Officer Amendment 210 
Act of 2025”. 211 
 Sec. 1032. The Office of the Chief Technology Officer Establishment Act of 1998, 212 
effective March 26, 1999 (D.C. Law 12-175; D.C. Official Code § 1-1401 et seq.), is amended as 213 
follows: 214 
 (a) Section 1812a (D.C. Official Code § 1-1401.01) is amended to read as follows: 215 
 “Sec. 1812a. Definitions. 216 
 “For the purposes of this act, the term: 217 
 “(a) “Information technology” includes artificial intelligence. 218 
 “(b) “Office” means the Office of the Chief Technology Officer established by section 219 
1812.”.  220 
 (b) Section 1814 (D.C. Official Code § 1-1403) is amended as follows: 221 
  (1) Paragraph (1) is amended by striking the phrase “Issue regulations governing 222 
the acquisition, use, and management” and inserting the phrase “Issue and enforce policies, 223 
standards, and regulations governing the acquisition, use, management, and security” in its place. 224 
  (2) New paragraphs (7A), (7B), and (7C) are added to read as follows: 225 
  “(7A) Protect the confidentiality, integrity, and availability of the District 226 
government’s information technology systems and assets; 227 
  “(7B) Protect the District government’s information technology systems and 228 
assets, and the information on those systems and assets, from cyberattacks, breaches, theft, 229 
damage, disruption, and misdirection; 230

11 
  “(7C) Detect, mitigate, defend, remediate, and respond to cybersecurity threats 231 
and security vulnerabilities in the District government’s information technology systems and 232 
assets;”. 233 
  (3) The lead-in language of paragraph (12) is amended by striking the phrase 234 
“paragraph (10)” and inserting the phrase “paragraph (11)” in its place. 235 
 (c) Section 1816(a) (D.C. Official Code § 1-1405(a)) is amended as follows: 236 
  (1) The lead-in language is amended by striking the number “3” and inserting the 237 
number “4” in its place. 238 
  (2) Paragraph (2) is amended by striking the phrase “; and” and inserting a 239 
semicolon in its place. 240 
  (3) Paragraph (3) is amended by striking the period and inserting the phrase “; 241 
and” in its place. 242 
  (4) A new paragraph (4) is added to read as follows: 243 
  “(4) Security Services, which shall be responsible for: 244 
   “(A) Leading the District government’s cybersecurity efforts, including 245 
managing the risk of cyberattacks and breaches, and detecting, mitigating, defending, and 246 
responding to cybersecurity threats and security vulnerabilities in the District government’s 247 
network and systems; and  248 
   “(B) Establishing an information technology risk management and 249 
compliance program throughout the District government that may include governance, 250 
development, implementation, and management of a formal process for systems authorization 251 
that includes a risk assessment, categorization of information and systems, selection and 252

12 
implementation of controls, assessment of controls, authorization to operate, and continuous 253 
monitoring.”. 254 
 (d) Section 1816a (D.C. Official Code § 1-1406) is amended to read as follows: 255 
 “Sec. 1816a. Jurisdiction. 256 
 “(a) Except as provided in subsection (b) of this section, the authority of the Office shall 257 
apply to all District government agencies, including independent agencies. 258 
 “(b)(1) The authority of the Office under sections 1813 and 1814 shall not apply to the 259 
Council or the Office of the District of Columbia Auditor. 260 
  “(2) Notwithstanding paragraph (1) of this subsection, the Office and the Council 261 
and the Office and the Office of the District of Columbia Auditor may enter into written 262 
agreements pursuant to which the Office may: 263

and 266 
   “(B) Carry out any other responsibility for the Council or the Office of the 267 
District of Columbia Auditor that the Office may carry out for other District government 268 
agencies.”. 269 
SUBTITLE E. TELEWORK POLICIES 270 
 Sec. 1041. Short title. 271 
 This subtitle may be cited as the “Telework Policy Amendment Act of 2025”. 272 
 Sec. 1042. The District of Columbia Government Comprehensive Merit Personnel Act of 273 
1978, effective March 3, 1979 (D.C. Law 2-139; D.C. Official Code § 1-601.01 et seq.), is 274 
amended by adding a new section 201a to read as follows: 275

13 
 “Sec. 201a. Telework. 276 
 “(a) The Mayor may establish a telework policy that applies to all agencies. 277 
 “(b) No personnel authority or agency shall establish its own telework policy, unless 278 
authorized by the Mayor, and no personnel authority or agency shall implement a telework 279 
policy other than the policy established by the Mayor pursuant to subsection (a) of this section, 280 
unless authorized by the Mayor. 281 
 “(c) At the request of the Mayor, each agency shall submit a report to the Mayor that 282 
includes information on the use of telework by the agency’s employees. 283 
 “(d) The Mayor may audit agencies’ implementation of the telework policy established 284 
pursuant to subsection (a) or (b) of this section and employees’ utilization of telework to ensure 285 
compliance with the telework policy and this section. 286 
 “(e) No personnel authority or agency may enter into a collective bargaining agreement 287 
that includes or requires a telework policy. 288 
 “(f) For the purposes of this section, the term: 289 
  “(1) “Agency” shall have the meaning set forth in section 301(1) and shall include 290 
agencies not otherwise subject to this act, except for the Council and agencies of the legislative 291 
branch of the District government. 292 
  “(2) “Routine telework” means a telework arrangement in which an employee is 293 
authorized to telework on an ongoing basis. 294 
  “(3) “Situational telework” means a temporary telework arrangement in which the 295 
employee is authorized to telework due to specific, temporary personal circumstances which 296 
prevent the employee from working from a District government office or worksite or for another 297 
specific, temporary circumstance approved by the personnel authority. 298

14 
  “(4)(A) “Telework” means an arrangement in which an employee performs 299 
employment duties at their home or other location that is not a District government office or 300 
worksite during hours that constitute their official tour of duty. 301 
   “(B) The term “telework” includes both routine telework and situational 302 
telework.”. 303 
 “(g) The Mayor, pursuant to Title I of the District of Columbia Administrative Procedure 304 
Act, approved October 21, 1968 (82 Stat. 1204; D.C. Official Code § 2-501 et seq.), may issue 305 
rules to implement the provisions of this section.”. 306

  (1) Subsection (a)(1)(B) is amended by striking the phrase “8 workweeks” and 333

  (2) A new subsection (c-1) is added to read as follows: 335 
 “(c-1)(1) To the extent practicable, an eligible employee shall, before using paid leave, 336 
provide written notice to their personnel authority or agency of the need for the use of paid leave 337 
and of the expected date or dates on which the employee intends to use paid leave. 338 
  “(2) If the employee plans to use paid leave on an intermittent basis, the written 339 
notice shall, to the extent practicable, include a schedule of the expected hours during which the 340 
employee intends to use paid leave. 341

16 
  “(3) The written notice shall include a reason for the use of paid leave, within the 342 
parameters of the Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act of 1996, approved August 343 
21, 1996 (Pub. L. No. 104-191; 110 Stat. 1936). 344 
  “(4) If the use of paid leave is foreseeable, the written notice shall be provided at 345 
least 10 days, or as early as possible, in advance of the use of the paid leave. 346 
  “(5) If the use of paid leave is unforeseeable, a notification, either oral or written, 347 
shall be provided before the start of the work shift for which the paid leave is being used. 348 
  “(6) In the case of an emergency resulting in the use of paid leave without the 349 
opportunity to provide prior notification, the eligible employee, or another individual on behalf 350 
of the eligible employee, shall notify the Mayor, either orally or in writing, within 48 hours after 351 
the emergency occurs. 352 
  “(7) If the agency determines that the use of paid leave is likely to interfere with 353 
the operations of the agency, the agency and eligible employee shall engage in good-faith 354 
negotiations on alternate dates or hours for the use of the paid leave.  The agency may deny the 355 
use of paid leave under this section if an employee does not agree to a reasonable request to 356 
revise the dates or hours for the use of the paid leave.”. 357 
  (3) Subsection (d)(2) is amended to read as follows: 358 
  “(2) If a probationary employee voluntarily separates in violation of the 1-year 359 
continuation of service agreement, the individual shall be indebted to the District government for 360 
the salary paid during the leave period. Indebtedness incurred pursuant to this paragraph may be 361 
treated in whole or in part as an erroneous payment pursuant to Title XXIX.”. 362 
  (4) A new subsection (d-1) is added to read as follows: 363

17 
 “(d-1)(1) An employee, other than an employee serving in a probationary capacity, using 364 
paid parental, family, or medical leave shall be required to enter into a continuation of service 365 
agreement.  The continuation of service agreement shall require the employee to continue to 366 
serve as a District employee for 12 weeks after the use of the paid parental, family, or medical 367 
leave. 368 
  “(2) If the employee voluntarily separates in violation of the continuation of 369 
service agreement, the individual shall be indebted to the District government for the salary paid 370 
during the leave period. Indebtedness incurred pursuant to this paragraph may be treated in 371 
whole or in part as an erroneous payment pursuant to Title XXIX.”. 372

SUBTITLE I. RETROACTIVE PAY INCREASES 408 
 Sec. 1081. Short title.    409

19 
 This subtitle may be cited as the “Retroactive Pay Increase Restriction Amendment Act 410 
of 2025”.    411 
 Sec. 1082. Definitions. 412 
 For the purposes of this subtitle, the term “covered agency” means an agency, office, or 413 
instrumentality of the District government and independent agencies, as defined in section 414 
301(13) of the CMPA (D.C. Official Code § 1-603.01(13)); except, that the term “covered 415 
agency” does not include the District of Columbia Housing Authority, the District of Columbia 416 
Housing Finance Agency, the District of Columbia Water and Sewer Authority, the Not-for-417 
Profit Hospital Corporation, the University of the District of Columbia, or the Washington 418 
Convention and Sports Authority. 419 
 Sec. 1083. Restriction on certain retroactive pay increases. 420 
 Notwithstanding any other provision of law, rule, collective bargaining agreement, 421 
memorandum of understanding, side letter, settlement, or arbitration award, no increase in salary, 422 
wages, or benefits at a covered agency shall be authorized or provided retroactively for Fiscal 423 
Year 2024 or Fiscal Year 2025, except for: 424 
 (a) An increase provided for by a law enacted, resolution approved, or rule issued before 425 
May 15, 2025; 426 
 (b) An increase included in a salary, wage, or benefit provision that was included in: 427 
  (1) A collective bargaining agreement with respect to which the parties to the 428 
agreement reached agreement before May 15, 2025; or 429 
  (2) A memorandum of understanding or side letter entered into before May 15, 430 
2025; or 431

20 
 (c) An increase required by a final arbitration award that is not still subject to judicial 432 
review, if the arbitration was initiated before May 15, 2025; and 433 
 (d) An increase provided to an employee on an individualized basis pursuant to a law, 434 
resolution, rule, salary, wage, or benefit provision,  or arbitration award referred to in paragraph 435 
(2) of this subsection, based on the employee’s performance or another factor authorized by such 436 
law, resolution, rule, collective bargaining agreement, memorandum of understanding, side 437 
letter, settlement, or arbitration award. 438 
 Sec. 1084. Rules. 439 
 To the extent a personnel authority is authorized by the District of Columbia Government 440 
Comprehensive Merit Personnel Act of 1978, effective March 3, 1979 (D.C. Law 2-139; D.C. 441 
Official Code § 1-601.01 et seq.), or other law to issue rules to administer the salary or benefits 442 
program of a covered agency, the personnel authority for the covered agency may, pursuant to 443 
Title I of the District of Columbia Administrative Procedure Act, approved October 21, 1968 (82 444 
Stat. 1204; D.C. Official Code § 2-501 et seq.), issue rules to implement this subtitle with respect 445 
to the covered agency. 446 
 Sec. 1085. Applicability. 447 
 This subtitle shall apply as of May 15, 2025. 448

 (j) New sections 8a and 8b are added to read as follows: 794 
 “Sec. 8a. Seizure of unlawfully vended merchandise and associated vending property. 795 
 “(a) The Mayor may, upon issuance of a notice of infraction and seizure and without 796 
providing a prior opportunity to be heard, seize any merchandise being sold from public space, 797 
as well as any property, including vending carts and equipment, used to vend the merchandise, if: 798 
  “(1) The person: 799 
   “(A) Is vending the merchandise without a required authorization; 800 
   “(B) Fails to provide proof of a required authorization upon the request of 801 
the enforcement officer; or 802 
   “(C) Offers or provides to an enforcement officer an altered or falsified 803 
required authorization. 804 
  “(2) An altered or falsified required authorization is displayed at the location 805 
where the merchandise is being vended. 806 
 “(b)(1) If the Mayor seizes a person’s merchandise or property pursuant to subsection (a), 807 
the Mayor shall hand deliver the notice of infraction and seizure to the vendor, and the notice of 808

37 
infraction and seizure shall be treated for purposes of answering, hearing, adjudication, decision, 809 
and appeal as a notice of infraction under section 201 of the Department of Consumer and 810 
Regulatory Affairs Civil Infractions Act of 1985 (“Civil Infractions Act”), effective October 5, 811 
1985 (D.C. Law 6-42; D.C. Official Code § 2-1802.01). 812 
  “(2) If the person vending the merchandise is different than the individual listed in 813 
DLCP’s records as the business owner, the Mayor shall also provide the notice of infraction and 814 
seizure, by first-class mail, no later than 5 business days after property is seized, to the last 815 
known address of the business owner in DLCP’s records. If DLCP has no record of the business 816 
license owner, providing the notice of infraction and seizure to the person vending the 817 
merchandise shall be considered constructive notice to the owner of the merchandise. 818 
  “(3) A notice of infraction and seizure issued pursuant to paragraph (1) or (2) of 819 
this subsection shall include the information required by section 201(b) of the Civil Infractions 820 
Act (D.C. Official Code § 2-1802.01(b)) and the following additional information: 821 
   “(A) A description of the action taken by the Mayor, including a 822 
description of the seized merchandise and property; 823 
   “(B) A statement that the respondent has a right to request an expedited 824 
hearing by making a request for an expedited hearing in writing within 3 business days after 825 
service of the notice; 826 
   “(C) The method by which the respondent may recover the merchandise or 827 
property that was seized, if applicable; 828 
   “(D) The date by which the respondent must recover the merchandise or 829 
property before it may be subject to forfeiture and disposal pursuant to this section, which date 830 
shall be at least 21 days after the date of seizure, except where the merchandise or property is 831

38 
likely to perish or be greatly reduced in value after seizure or the expense of keeping the 832 
merchandise or property is disproportionate to its value;  833 
   “(E) A statement that the merchandise or property shall be considered 834 
forfeited and will be sold or otherwise disposed of if it is not recovered by the respondent within 835 
21 days of seizure; except as provided in subparagraph (F) of this paragraph; and 836 
   “(F) A statement that if the merchandise or property is likely to perish or 837 
be greatly reduced in value by or after its seizure, or the expense of keeping the merchandise or 838 
property is disproportionate to its value, the Mayor may immediately sell or otherwise dispose of 839 
the merchandise or property. 840 
 “(c) If a respondent requests an expedited hearing within 3 business days after service of 841 
the notice of infraction and seizure, the Office of Administrative Hearings shall conduct the 842 
hearing within 3 business days after receipt of the request and shall issue a decision within 10 843 
business days after the hearing. 844 
 “(d)(1) When merchandise or property is seized pursuant to this section, the Mayor shall: 845 
   “(A) Place the merchandise and property in an area designated by the 846 
Mayor for safe storage until disposition; 847 
   “(B) Create an inventory of the seized merchandise and property with a 848 
description of all merchandise and property seized; and 849 
   “(C) Assign an identification number to each item of merchandise and 850 
property seized, except as provided in paragraph (2) of this subsection. 851 
  “(2) For the purposes of this subsection: 852 
   “(A) A sum of currency shall be deemed to be a single item of property; 853 
and 854

39 
   “(B) Identical items may be identified by a single identification number; 855 
provided, that the quantity of the item is listed with the identification number. 856 
 “(e)(1) If seized merchandise or property is likely to perish or be greatly reduced in value 857 
by or after its seizure, or the expense of keeping the merchandise or property is disproportionate 858 
to its value, the Mayor may immediately sell or otherwise dispose of the merchandise or 859 
property. 860 
  “(2) If, pursuant to paragraph (1) of this subsection, the Mayor sells seized 861 
merchandise or property, the sale proceeds shall become substitute property for the original 862 
seized merchandise or property and shall be subject to forfeiture in accordance with the standards 863 
and procedures set forth in this section. 864 
  “(3) If, pursuant to paragraph (1) of this subsection, the Mayor discards seized 865 
property, the fair market value of the discarded property before it is discarded shall become 866

  “(4) The Mayor shall maintain records of merchandise and property sold or 869

 “(f) The vendor, or the owner if different from the vendor, whose merchandise or 873

infractions giving rise to the seizure are dismissed or overturned and such dismissal or 882

 “Sec. 8b. Towing and impoundment of unlawfully operating vending trucks. 909 
 “(a)(1) The Mayor may, upon issuance of a notice of infraction and impoundment and 910 
without providing a prior opportunity to be heard, seize, tow, and impound: 911 
   “(A) A vending truck if the vending truck owner, vending truck operator, 912

required authorization; 917 
    “(iii) Fails to provide proof of a required authorization upon 918 
request by an enforcement officer; or 919 
    “(iv) Operates the vending truck illegally in public space; or 920

42 
   “(B) A vending truck that: 921 
    “(i) Is illegally parked in public space; or 922 
    “(ii) Displays an altered or falsified required authorization. 923 
   “(C) A vehicle that is illegally parked in a vending truck vending zone. 924 
  “(2) If towing services are not immediately available, the Mayor may immobilize 925 
the vehicle until towing services are available. 926 
 “(b)(1) If the Mayor impounds a vending truck under this subsection, the Mayor shall, as 927 
further described in this subsection, provide a written notice of infraction and impoundment to: 928 
   “(A) The vending truck owner; 929 
   “(B) The vending truck operator if different than the food truck owner; 930 
and 931 
   “(C) Any lienholders of record of the vending truck, if applicable. 932 
  “(2) A notice of infraction and impoundment issued pursuant to this section shall 933 
be treated for purposes of answering, hearing, adjudication, decision, and appeal as a notice of 934 
infraction under section 201 of the Department of Consumer and Regulatory Affairs Civil 935 
Infractions Act of 1985 (“Civil Infractions Act”), effective October 5, 1985 (D.C. Law 6-42; 936 
D.C. Official Code § 2-1802.01). 937 
  “(3) A notice of infraction and impoundment issued pursuant to this section shall 938 
include the information required by section 201(b) of the Civil Infractions Act (D.C. Official 939

   “(A) The vehicle identification number of the vending truck, if applicable; 941 
   “(B) The amount of any fees imposed or to be imposed; 942

   “(C) A statement that the vending truck will be kept for 72 hours and a 943

   “(D) The date by which the person must reclaim the vending truck before 948

   “(F) A statement that the person has the right to request an expedited 955

  “(4)(A) The maximum rates that may be charged for the towing of a vending 958 
truck pursuant to this section shall be as follows: 959 
    “(i) $750 for preparation, hoist, and tow (including use of a roll-960 
back or wheel lift) to a location within the District; 961 
    “(ii) $20 per mile for towing beyond the District line; and 962 
    “(iii) $75 for storage per 24-hour period or part thereof. 963 
   “(B) Upon written request, and if approved by the Director of the 964 
Department of Licensing and Consumer Protection, the private tow company employed to 965

remove a vending truck pursuant to this section may charge a towing fee greater than what is set 966 
forth in this subsection, but no greater than three times the amount.  967

 Sec. 2061. Short title. 1163 
 This subtitle may be cited as the “Poker and Blackjack Gaming Authorization Act of 1164

for Charitable Purposes in the District of Columbia, effective March 10, 1981 (D.C. Law 3-172; 1167 
D.C. Official Code § 36-601.01 passim), is amended as follows: 1168

 (a) Section 3 (D.C. Official Code § 22-1716) is amended by striking the phrase “and 1169

 (c) A new title V is added to read as follows: 1174 
 “TITLE V. CARD GAMING; POKER AND BLACKJACK. 1175 
 “Sec. 501 Definitions. 1176 
 “For the purposes of this title the term: 1177 
  “(1) “ABCA” means the Alcoholic Beverage and Cannabis Administration. 1178 
  “(2) “ABCB” means the Alcoholic Beverage and Cannabis Board. 1179 
  “(3) “Authorized establishment” means an eligible establishment where a card 1180 
gaming facility is authorized to operate under this title. 1181 
  “(4) “Blackjack gross gaming revenue” means the total amount wagered by 1182 
players on blackjack, including any variations thereof, less total winnings paid out to players, not 1183 
including promotions, credits or other noncash items. 1184 
  “(5) “Card game” means blackjack or poker, including any authorized variations 1185 
of blackjack or poker.  1186 
  “(6) “Card gaming” means wagering on a card game. 1187 
  “(7) “Card gaming facility” means a location where wagering on card games is 1188 
authorized under this title.  1189 
  “(8) “Card gaming operations” means the operation of an authorized card gaming 1190 
facility. 1191

54 
“(9) “CFO” means the Chief Financial Officer of the District of Columbia. 1192 
  “(10) “Eligible establishment” means a business licensed by the Alcoholic 1193 
Beverage and Cannabis Board that holds an on-premises retailer’s license class C/H, D/H or 1194 
arena CX. 1195 
  “(11) “Office” means the Office of Lottery and Gaming. 1196 
  “(12) “Operator” means a person who holds a card gaming facility operator 1197 
license. 1198 
  “(13) “Person” means a natural person or entity. 1199 
  “(14) “Poker gross gaming revenue” means the gross receipts generated from 1200 
poker, including any variations thereof, including rake, entry fees, commissions, and similar 1201 
amounts paid by players to participate in poker games. 1202 
  “(15) “Supplier” means a person that: 1203 
   “(A) Sells or leases, or contracts to sell or lease, card game equipment, 1204 
devices, systems, or supplies to an operator; or 1205 
   “(B) Provides, or contracts to provide, security or surveillance services 1206 
required by the Office to an operator; 1207 
   “(C) Provides management services to an operator.  1208 
  “(16) “Voluntary exclusion program” means a program operated by the Office 1209 
that allows individuals to voluntarily exclude themselves from engaging in card gaming.  1210 
 “Sec 502. Authorization of card gaming. 1211 
 “The operation of facilities where wagering on card games occurs, and the wagering on 1212 
card games at such facilities, shall be lawful in the District if conducted in accordance with this 1213 
title and the rules issued pursuant to this title. 1214

55 
 “Sec. 503. Card gaming locations. 1215 
 “(a) Card gaming shall be permitted under this title only at locations authorized by the 1216 
Office to be operated as card gaming facilities. 1217 
 “(b) The Office shall authorize card gaming facilities to be operated only at eligible 1218 
establishments. 1219 
 “(c) Card gaming facilities shall be authorized to be operated only as part of an operator’s 1220 
license issued under this title. 1221 
 “(d) The Office shall, by rule, establish a limit on the number of card gaming facilities 1222 
that may be authorized to be operated in the District.  In determining this limit, the Office shall 1223 
consider the sufficiency of the number of card gaming facilities to serve the public. 1224 
 “Sec. 504. Operator’s license required; fee. 1225 
 “(a) No person shall operate a card gaming facility unless the person holds an operator’s 1226 
license issued by the Office.  An operator’s license shall authorize the licensee to operate card 1227 
gaming facilities only at such eligible establishments as shall be authorized on the license. An 1228 
operator shall not operate a card gaming facility at a location in the District other than an eligible 1229 
establishment authorized on its license. 1230 
 “(b) To obtain an operator’s license a person shall: 1231 
  “(1) Complete and submit to the Office an application for an operator’s license in 1232 
the manner and form prescribed by the Office; 1233 
  “(2) Submit any information and documents requested by the Office as part of the 1234 
licensing process; 1235 
  “(3) Satisfactorily pass a background investigation, which may include the 1236 
investigation of owners, officers, directors and employees of the applicant; 1237

56 
  “(4) Satisfactorily pass an onsite inspection of the location or locations where the 1238 
applicant proposes to conduct card gaming operations;  1239 
  “(5) Meet such other standards as the Office may establish by rule; and 1240 
  “(6) Pay a nonrefundable application fee of $5,000, plus $2,000 for each location 1241 
where the applicant proposes to conduct card gaming operations. 1242 
 “(c) The term of an operator’s license shall be two years. 1243 
 “(d) A licensee may renew an operator’s license by filing an application with the Office 1244 
and providing such information and documents as may be requested by the Office. The 1245 
application fee for the renewal of an operator’s license shall be $1,500 plus $500 for each 1246 
location where the licensee is currently licensed to operate a card gaming facility. 1247 
 “(e) As part of a renewal application, a licensee may request authorization to conduct 1248 
card gaming operations at additional locations.  The applicant shall pay an additional application 1249 
fee of $1,000 for each new location requested. 1250 
 “(f) An operator’s license shall not be transferable to another person, unless approved by 1251 
the Office in accordance with rules issued by the Office. 1252 
 “(g) A person convicted of a disqualifying offense shall not be licensed as an operator by 1253 
the Office. The Office shall define disqualifying offense by a rule issued pursuant to this title. 1254 
 “(h) No employee of the Office or ABCA or member of the ABC Board, or immediate 1255 
family member of an employee of the Office or ABCA or member of the ABC Board, may be an 1256 
applicant for, have an interest in, or obtain an operator’s license.”. 1257 
 “(i) A person who has been denied an application for an operator’s license pursuant to 1258 
this section or rules issued pursuant this title shall have a right to a hearing before the Office and, 1259

57 
in the event of its affirmation of the denial, the right to appeal the decision of the Office to the 1260 
Superior Court of the District of Columbia. 1261 
 “(j) Before issuing, renewing, or authorizing the transfer a license, the Office shall 1262 
determine that the applicant is not disqualified because of a conflicting interest in another license 1263 
or a supplier permit.  The Office shall define conflicting interest and the standards for 1264 
disqualification by rules issued pursuant to this title. 1265 
 “Sec. 505. Designated areas for card gaming operations within authorized establishments. 1266 
 “(a) Each licensee shall locate its card gaming operations within the premises of an 1267 
authorized establishment only in specific areas approved by ABCA and the Office. 1268 
 “(b) Each licensee shall post a warning sign clearly visible at each point of entry to the 1269 
designated areas where card gaming is located in an authorized establishment. The warning sign 1270 
shall include: 1271 
  “(1) The minimum age required to participate in card gaming; 1272 
  “(2) The contact information for the District’s gambling hotline; 1273 
  “(3) Information on the voluntary exclusion program; and 1274 
  “(4) The contact information for the Office for purposes of filing a licensee or 1275 
card gaming facility. 1276 
 “Sec. 506. Card gaming by minors prohibited. 1277 
 “(a) Neither a licensee nor an authorized establishment shall permit a person under the 1278 
age of 18 to participate in card gaming. 1279 
 “(b) The Office may suspend or revoke a license and issue a fine, in accordance with 1280 
section XXX, against a licensee that knowingly allows a person under the age of 18 to participate 1281 
in card gaming. 1282

58 
 “Sec. 507. Voluntary exclusion program. 1283 
 “Each licensee shall participate in the voluntary exclusion program operated by the 1284 
Office. 1285 
 “Sec. 508. Taxation of card gaming. 1286 
 “(a) A tax is imposed on all operators licensed by the Office for the operation of a card 1287 
gaming facility. 1288 
 “(b) The rate of tax shall be 25% of the poker gross gaming revenue and 25% of the 1289 
blackjack gross gaming revenue from each card gaming facility the operator is authorized to 1290 
operate. 1291 
 “(c) On or before the 20th calendar day of each month, each operator shall file a return 1292 
with the CFO, on forms and in the manner prescribed by the CFO, indicating the amount of 1293 
poker gross gaming revenue and blackjack gross gaming revenue from the operator’s authorized 1294 
card gaming facilities for the preceding calendar month and the amount of tax for which the 1295 
operator is liable. 1296 
 “(d) All funds owed to the District under this section shall be held in trust for the District 1297 
in a federally insured depository institution that maintains an office in the District until the funds 1298 
are paid to the District. 1299 
 “(e) Each operator shall keep a record of the poker gross gaming revenue and blackjack 1300 
gross gaming revenue in such form as the CFO may require. 1301 
 “(f) An operator who fails to pay the tax imposed by this section shall be subject to all 1302 
collection, enforcement, and administrative provisions applicable to unpaid taxes or fees, as 1303 
provided in Chapters 41, 42, 43, and 44 of Title 47 of the District of Columbia Official Code. 1304

59 
 “(g) Notwithstanding section 47-4406 of the District of Columbia Official Code, the CFO 1305 
may disclose the total amount of poker gross gaming revenue and blackjack gross gaming 1306 
revenue collected in the periodic estimates and reports of revenues. 1307 
 “Sec. 509. Rules governing card gaming. 1308 
 “(a) The CFO, pursuant to title I of the District of Columbia Administrative Procedure 1309 
Act, approved October 21, 1968 (82 Stat. 1204; D.C. Official Code § 2-501 et seq.), shall adopt 1310 
rules governing the conditions under which card gaming shall be conducted in the District and 1311 
such other rules it deems appropriate to implement or further the purposes of this title. 1312 
 “(b) The rules issued by the CFO pursuant to subsection (a) of this section shall include: 1313 
  “(1) Minimum internal control standards, including: 1314 
   “(A) Financial controls, including rules regarding the proper handling of 1315 
cash, accounting procedures, and auditing requirements; 1316 
   “(B) Security and surveillance requirements, including rules regarding the 1317 
monitoring of gaming areas, protecting against theft and cheating, surveillance camera standards, 1318 
and recording and monitoring requirements; 1319 
   “(C) Game integrity standards designed to ensure fair play, accurate 1320 
payouts, and compliance with card game rules; 1321 
   “(D) Employee-related standards and requirements, including background 1322 
checks, training, and ethical conduct requirements; and 1323 
   “(E) Information technology and data security standards and requirements 1324 
designed to protect player information and ensure system integrity. 1325 
  “(2) Minimum and maximum wager amounts; 1326 
   “(3) The method of accounting to be used by operators; 1327

60 
 “(4) Requirements and methods of age verification; 1328 
  “(5) Requirements regarding records required to be maintained by an operator; 1329 
  “(6) Advertising guidelines, including specific language concerning individuals 1330 
under the age of 18; and 1331 
  “(7) Penalties for a violation of this title or rule issued pursuant to this title.”. 1332 
 “Sec. 510. Supplier permit. 1333 
 “(a) No person shall operate as a supplier unless the person holds a supplier permit issued 1334 
by the Office. 1335 
 “(b) The Office shall establish by rule the requirements and standards for issuance of a 1336 
supplier permit and may establish a fee for the application for and issuance of a supplier permit. 1337 
 “(c) No employee of the Office or ABCA or member of the ABC Board, or immediate 1338 
family member of an employee of the Office or ABCA or member of the ABC Board, may be an 1339 
applicant for, have an interest in, or obtain a supplier’s permit.”. 1340 
 “Sec. 511. Penalties. 1341 
 “(a) For a violation of this title or a rule issued pursuant to this title, the Office may take 1342 
one or more of the following actions: 1343 
  “(1) Impose a fine of not more than $50,000; 1344 
  “(2) Revoke an operator’s license or a supplier’s permit; or 1345 
  “(3) Suspend an operator’s license or supplier’s permit. 1346 
 “(b) A person who has been fined or whose license has been revoked or suspended 1347 
pursuant to this section shall have a right to a hearing before the Office and, in the event of its 1348 
affirmation of the fine, revocation or suspension, whichever applies, the right to appeal the 1349 
decision of the Office to the Superior Court of the District of Columbia. 1350

61 
 “Sec. 512. Audits, investigations, and inspections. 1351 
 “(a) The Office may conduct audits, investigations, searches, seizures, and other 1352 
investigatory and inspection activities appropriate to the implementation and enforcement of this 1353 
title and the rules issued pursuant to this title. 1354 
 “(b) Each applicant for a license, each licensee, each authorized establishment and each 1355 
eligible establishment that is seeking to become an authorized establishment shall allow an 1356 
authorized member of the Office, an ABCA investigator, or any member of the Metropolitan 1357 
Police Department full opportunity to examine at any time during business hours: 1358 
  “(1) The area of the authorized establishment or eligible establishment where card 1359 
gaming operations are conducted or are requested to be authorized to be conducted; and 1360 
  “(2) The books and records of the applicant, licensee, authorized establishment, or 1361 
eligible establishment, including video and audio recordings, wherever stored. 1362 
 “Sec. 513. Other enforcement authority; seizures. 1363 
 “(a) The Office, ABCA, or Metropolitan Police Department may request and check the 1364 
identification of a person who has played, is playing, or is attempting to play a card game. The 1365 
Office or Metropolitan Police Department may seize evidence that substantiates a violation under 1366 
this title, which may include seizing the cash awards issued to a person under the age of 18 and 1367 
fake identification documents used by a person under the age of 18. 1368 
 “(b) The Office or Metropolitan Police Department may seize card gaming equipment, 1369 
devices, systems, or supplies from a business if: 1370 
  “(1) A card gaming facility is not authorized to be operated in the business; 1371 
  “(2) The license of the operator allowing the operator to operate a card gaming 1372 
facility at the business has been suspended, revoked, or canceled by the Office; 1373

62 
  “(3) The business is no longer in existence; or 1374 
  “(4) The business has been closed by another District government agency. 1375 
 “Sec. 514. Action by the Attorney General 1376 
 The Attorney General for the District of Columbia, in the name of the District of 1377 
Columbia, may bring an action in the Superior Court of the District of Columbia to enjoin a 1378 
person from violating this title or a rule issued pursuant to this title or to seek a civil penalty of 1379 
up to $50,000 for a violation of this title or a rule issued pursuant to this title. 1380 
 “Sec. 515. Other powers and duties of the Office. 1381 
 “In addition to the other authorities of the Office under this title, the Office shall have the 1382 
power to: 1383 
 “(a) Oversee all card gaming operations authorized under this title, including all persons 1384 
conducting or participating in any card gaming operation; 1385 
 “(b) Employ or contract for such persons to be present during card gaming operations as 1386 
are necessary to ensure that such gaming operations are conducted in an orderly fashion and the 1387 
highest degree of integrity; and 1388 
 “(c) Issue subpoenas for the attendance of witnesses before the Office, administer oaths, 1389 
and compel production of records or other documents and testimony of such witnesses whenever 1390 
in the judgment of the Office it is necessary to do so for the effectual discharge of its duties 1391 
under this title.”. 1392

 (e) A new subchapter IX-A is added to read as follows: 1441 
 “Subchapter IX-A. Game of skill machines and card gaming. 1442

65 
 “§ 25-788. Game of skill machine operating requirements. 1443 
 “A licensee with a game of skill machine endorsement shall: 1444 
  “(1) Not allow or permit a person under 18 years of age to play a game of skill 1445 
machine and shall designate an employee to regularly monitor the designated area where game of 1446 
skill machines are played to ensure that no person under 18 years of age is playing or attempting 1447 
to play a game of skill machine; 1448 
  “(2) Verify that each person playing a game of skill machine is lawfully permitted 1449 
to do so by checking the person’s government-issued identification document upon entry into 1450 
either the licensed establishment or the designated area where the game of skill machines are 1451 
located and where the person seeks to cash out his or her winnings, if any; except, that the failure 1452 
of a licensee to verify a person’s identification shall not be a violation of this paragraph if the 1453 
person whose identification was not checked is 18 years of age or older; 1454 
  “(3) Not allow or permit a person that appears intoxicated or under the influence 1455 
of a narcotic or other substance to play a game of skill machine; 1456 
  “(4) Not share revenue from the licensee’s sale of alcohol with a manufacturer or 1457 
distributor of a game of skill machine, unless approved by the Board as an owner of the license; 1458 
  “(5) Not allow or permit the placement of a game of skill machine on an outdoor 1459 
public or private space that has not been approved by the Board; 1460 
  “(6) Not allow or permit the placement of a game of skill machine outside of the 1461 
designated areas contained on the applicant’s diagram provided as part of the license application 1462 
or outside the areas approved by the Board; 1463 
  “(7) Not have more than 5 game of skill machines on the licensed premises; and 1464

66 
  “(8) Install security cameras that are operational and record for 30 days, in the 1465 
areas designated for game of skill machines, near the cash register or terminal where cash 1466 
winnings of game of skill machines are processed, and where the licensee’s money is stored. 1467 
 “§ 25-789. Card gaming operating requirements. 1468 
 “A licensee with a card gaming endorsement shall: 1469 
  “(1) Not allow or permit a person under 18 years of age to participate in card 1470 
gaming and shall designate an employee to regularly monitor the designated area where card 1471 
gaming is located to ensure that no person under 18 years of age is participating or attempting to 1472 
participate in card gaming; 1473 
  “(2) Verify that each person participating in card gaming is lawfully permitted to 1474 
do so by checking the person’s government-issued identification document upon entry into either 1475 
the licensed establishment or the designated area where card gaming is located and where the 1476 
person seeks to cash out his or her winnings, if any; except, that the failure of a licensee to verify 1477 
a person’s identification shall not be a violation of this paragraph if the person whose 1478 
identification was not checked is 18 years of age or older; 1479 
  “(3) Not allow or permit a person that appears intoxicated or under the influence 1480 
of a narcotic or other substance to participate in card gaming; 1481 
  “(4) Not share revenue from the licensee’s sale of alcohol with a card gaming 1482 
operator, unless approved by the Board as an owner of the license; 1483 
  “(5) Not allow or permit card gaming outside of the areas approved by the Board; 1484 
  “(6) Install security cameras that are operational and record for 30 days, in the 1485 
areas designated for card gaming, near the cash register or terminal where cash winnings of card 1486 
gaming are processed, and where the licensee’s money is stored. 1487

 “(a-1)(1) Judicial review under this section of an order or decision of the Zoning 1852

Adjustment, or the Mayor’s Agent to the person, 30 days after the date the decision or order is 1865

    “(iv) The particularized harm is capable of being redressed by a 1878 
favorable decision by the Court. 1879 
  “(2) The Court shall not have jurisdiction under subsection (a) of this section of a 1880 
petition for judicial review of an order or decision of the Zoning Commission, the Board of 1881 
Zoning Adjustment, or the Mayor’s Agent that does not satisfy the requirements set forth in 1882 
paragraph (1) of this subsection. 1883 
  “(3)(A) The Court may, upon the motion of a party or intervenor in the appeal 1884 
who is the person in whose favor the order or decision in the contested case was issued (the 1885 
“prevailing party in the zoning decision”), require a person who filed a petition for judicial 1886 
review covered by this subsection (the “petitioner”) to file a bond of sufficient surety in an 1887 
amount the Court considers proper, not to exceed $250,000, to indemnify the prevailing party in 1888 
the zoning decision against damages which such party may sustain due to delays caused by the 1889 
appeal; provided, that the likelihood and estimated amount of damages is substantiated in the 1890 
motion.  In deciding whether to require such a bond and determining the amount of the bond, if 1891 
any, the Court shall consider the goal of discouraging frivolous and vexatious appeals, the goal 1892 
of not discouraging meritorious appeals, the extent to which the delays caused by the appeal may 1893

85 
harm the public interest, the ability of the petitioner to pay the bond and the financial burden of 1894 
the payment relative to the petitioner’s means, and the apparent meritoriousness of the appeal. 1895 
   “(B) If the order or decision in the contested case is affirmed by the Court, 1896 
the Court may, if a bond was required under subparagraph (A) of this paragraph and upon the 1897 
motion of the prevailing party in the zoning decision, order the petitioner to pay to the prevailing 1898 
party in the zoning decision an amount the Court considers proper to compensate the prevailing 1899 
party in the zoning decision for the damages it sustained due to delays caused by the appears; 1900 
provided, that the amount ordered to be paid shall not exceed $250,000.  In determining whether 1901 
to order such a payment, and the amount of such payment to award, if any, the Court shall 1902 
consider the extent to which the prevailing party in the zoning decision has substantiated that it 1903 
sustained damages due to delays caused by the appeal, whether the delays caused by the appeal 1904 
harmed the public interest, the goal of discouraging frivolous and vexatious appeals, the goal of 1905 
not discouraging meritorious appeals, the ability of the petitioner to make the payment and the 1906 
financial burden of the payment relative to the petitioner’s means, and the meritoriousness of the 1907 
appeal. 1908 
  “(4) If the order or decision is affirmed by the Court and the Court finds that the 1909 
appeal was frivolous or filed for an improper purpose, such as to harass or to cause unnecessary 1910 
delay, the Court may order the petitioner to reimburse to the prevailing party in the zoning 1911 
decision reasonable attorney’s fees and other costs incurred by it in defending against the appeal 1912 
in an amount not to exceed $50,000. 1913 
  “(4) For the purposes of this subsection, the term “Mayor’s Agent” means a 1914 
person designated by the Mayor to issue orders or decisions in contested cases pursuant to the 1915 
Historic Landmark and Historic District Protection Act of 1978, effective March 3, 1979 (D.C. 1916

86 
Law 2-144; D.C. Official Code § 6-1101 et seq.), or the individual to whom such person has 1917 
delegated the authority to issue orders or decisions in such contested cases.”. 1918

 Sec. 2173. Findings. 2013 
 (a) The RFK Campus consists of approximately 180 acres of waterfront property located 2014 
in the District within Reservation 343F, as shown on the transfer of jurisdiction plat recorded in 2015 
the Surveyor’s Office on February 5, 2025, on page 13 of subdivision book 223. The District has 2016 
been unable to redevelop the site over the last decade due to federal restrictions on development 2017 
at the site, and the site has remained vacant, blighted, and fallow. Over the course of the last 2018 
decade, the District has worked to gain broader control of the site and remove the federal 2019 
development restrictions so that a new, multi-use future for the area could be realized. 2020 
 (b) With bipartisan support from Congress, the President signed Public Law 118-274, the 2021 
D.C. Robert F. Kennedy Memorial Stadium Campus Revitalization Act, into law on January 6, 2022 
2025. Pursuant to this law, the National Park Service transferred administrative jurisdiction over 2023 
the RFK Campus to the District. The Council approved the transfer of jurisdiction pursuant to the 2024 
Transfer of Jurisdiction Over the Robert F. Kennedy Memorial Stadium Campus Emergency 2025 
Approval Resolution of 2025, effective February 4, 2025 (Res. 26-16; 72 DCR 1436). 2026 
 (c) The District now has a once-in-a-generation opportunity to continue to revitalize the 2027 
Anacostia Waterfront, catalyze growth, and develop a vibrant, mixed-use development featuring 2028 
community sports, professional sports, public recreation, public park space, and cultural 2029

91 
amenities. The development will provide the District with significant economic, cultural, social, 2030 
and financial benefits. 2031 
 (d) Since gaining administrative jurisdiction over the RFK Campus, the District 2032 
government has undertaken a comprehensive public engagement process, considering the 2033 
specific interests of communities near the RFK Campus; economic development opportunities; 2034 
the capacity of the campus to generate jobs for District residents and opportunities for District 2035 
businesses; park, open space, and recreational options; river access options; potential community 2036 
amenities; opportunities to create new housing units; and environmental and historical factors, 2037 
among other considerations, to determine the best uses for the site. 2038 
 (e) Considering the public input received, the existing infrastructure of the campus, and 2039 
the possibility of a catalytic investment in the District, it was determined that the construction 2040 
and operation of a new first-class, state-of-the-art, sports stadium with supporting facilities, 2041 
including parking, and infrastructure, for use by the professional football franchise owned by 2042 
Pro-Football LLC, and an adjacent Sportsplex would be of considerable public benefit to the 2043 
District as the first stage of the redevelopment and revitalization of the entire campus. 2044 
 (f) To ensure delivery of the Stadium, supporting facilities, and Sportsplex by 2030, a 2045 
limited zoning exemption is necessary for the RFK Campus. 2046 
 (g) Planning for other residential and commercial development at the RFK Campus, in 2047 
addition to other uses permitted by the transfer of jurisdiction and the Declaration of Covenants, 2048 
will continue as the Stadium and Sportsplex projects move forward, and will inform the 2049 
Comprehensive Plan rewrite (DC 2050). 2050 
 (h) The District intends for the comprehensive redevelopment of the RFK Campus to 2051 
create a vibrant, multi-use community and destination that will create new jobs, business 2052

92 
opportunities, housing, community amenities, and neighborhood-serving retail and achieve the 5 2053 
Anacostia Waterfront Framework Plan themes: (1) a clean and active river, (2) gaining access to, 2054 
along, and across the river, (3) a great riverfront park system, (4) a riverfront of distinct places 2055 
and cultural destinations, and (5) building and sustaining strong waterfront neighborhoods. 2056 
 (i) The District and Pro-Football LLC have entered into a term sheet for the development 2057 
of the Stadium and supporting facilities and the redevelopment of a portion of the RFK Campus 2058 
that sets forth the terms and conditions upon which the parties are willing to undertake the 2059 
projects. 2060 
 (j) The construction, development, and revitalization of the RFK Campus and the public 2061 
ownership of the Stadium, for use for athletic and entertainment events, is a municipal use that is 2062 
in the interest of, and for the benefit of, District residents because such a publicly-owned 2063 
Stadium will contribute to the social and economic well-being of District residents and 2064 
significantly enhance economic development and employment opportunities in the District. 2065 
 (k) To further that interest, it is appropriate for the District to take certain actions with 2066 
respect to the redevelopment of the RFK Campus, as further set forth in this act. 2067

secured; and 2279 
  (14) The selection of the underwriter for the bonds.  2280

  (1) Make and enter into any and all covenants and agreements with the trustee or 2299

except for estate, inheritance, and gift taxes. 2322 
 (k) The District hereby pledges and covenants and agrees with the holders of the bonds 2323 
that, subject to the provisions of the financing documents, the District will not limit or alter the 2324 
revenues pledged to secure the bonds or the basis on which the revenues are collected or 2325

allocated, will not impair the contractual obligations of the District to fulfill the terms of any 2326

exemptions from taxation provided for in this title, until the bonds, together with interest thereon, 2329 
with interest on any unpaid installment of interest and all costs and expenses in connection with 2330 
any suit, action, or proceeding by or on behalf of the holders of the bonds, are fully met and 2331 
discharged. This pledge and agreement for the District may be included as part of the contract 2332 
with the holders of the bonds. This subsection shall constitute a contract between the District and 2333 
the holders of the bonds. To the extent that any acts or resolutions of the Council may be in 2334 
conflict with this title, this title shall be controlling. 2335

Article 9 of Subtitle I of Title 28 of the District of Columbia Official Code: 2337 
  (1) A pledge made and security interest created in respect of the bonds or pursuant 2338

SUBTITLE E. DISTRICT OF COLUMBIA RAP BACK PROGRAM 3612 
 Sec. 3041. Short title.  3613 
 This subtitle may be cited as the “District of Columbia Rap Back Program Act of 2025”. 3614 
 Sec. 3042. Definitions.  3615 
 For the purposes of this subtitle, the term: 3616 
  (1) “Covered individual” means an individual who has submitted to a fingerprint-3617 
based background check as a condition of caring for children, persons with a disability, the 3618 
elderly, or vulnerable adults, protecting property, preventing theft, damage to real or personal 3619 
property, assaults, disorders, or other illegal occurrences, or for purposes of licensure, 3620 
appointment, commission, certification, registration, employment, or volunteer service with a 3621 
participating entity pursuant to District law or regulation and who has been subscribed by a 3622 
participating entity in the District of Columbia Rap Back Program.  3623 
  (2) “FBI” means the Federal Bureau of Investigation. 3624 
  (3) “FBI Rap Back Service” means the FBI Next Generation Identification 3625 
Record of Arrest and Prosecution Background Service. 3626

162 
  (4) “Notification event” means an event, such as an arrest or other criminal 3627 
activity where fingerprints are taken, that triggers a notification from the FBI Rap Back Service 3628 
to the Metropolitan Police Department regarding a covered individual who has been subscribed 3629 
to the FBI Rap Back Service. 3630 
  (5) “Participating agency” means a District government agency that requires a 3631 
fingerprint-based background check as a condition of caring for children, persons with a 3632 
disability, the elderly, vulnerable adults, protecting property, preventing theft, damage to real or 3633 
personal property, assaults, disorder, or similar illegal occurrences, or for purposes of licensure, 3634 
appointment, commission, certification, registration, employment, or volunteer service and that 3635 
has elected to subscribe covered individuals in the District of Columbia Rap Back Program. 3636 
 Sec. 3043. District of Columbia Rap Back Program. 3637 
 (a) The Metropolitan Police Department shall participate on behalf of the District in the 3638 
FBI Rap Back Service, through the establishment of a District of Columbia Rap Back Program. 3639 
 (b) The purpose of the District of Columbia Rap Back Program shall be to allow 3640 
participating agencies, through the District of Columbia Rap Back Program, to subscribe covered 3641 
individuals in the FBI Rap Back Service and to be notified when a covered individual is the 3642 
subject of an FBI Rap Back Service notification event. 3643 
 (c) A covered individual who will be subscribed by a participating agency in the FBI Rap 3644 
Back Service shall be fingerprinted by MPD. The fingerprints of the covered individual shall be 3645 
forwarded by MPD to the FBI for a national criminal history check and subscription in the FBI 3646 
Rap Back Service.  3647 
 (d) The Metropolitan Police Department shall notify the relevant participating agency 3648 
when a covered individual subscribed by the participating agency in the FBI Rap Back Service 3649

163 
through the District of Columbia Rap Back Program is the subject of a FBI Rap Back Service 3650 
notification event. 3651 
 (e) For the purposes of administering the District of Columbia Rap Back Program and 3652 
performing or facilitating criminal history background screenings, MPD is authorized to take the 3653 
fingerprints of a covered individual; submit to the FBI the fingerprints of, and accompanying 3654 
records regarding, a covered individual; and allow such fingerprints and records to be retained by 3655 
the FBI Rap Back Service and used for ongoing searches through the FBI Rap Back Service. 3656 
 Sec. 3044. Limitation on use of information. 3657 
 The information contained in a notification event received by a participating agency shall 3658 
be used by the participating entity only for the purpose of determining a covered individual’s 3659 
eligibility to care for children, persons with a disability, the elderly, or vulnerable adults, 3660 
protecting property, preventing theft, damage to real or personal property, assaults, disorders, or 3661 
similar illegal occurrences, or for purposes of licensure, appointment, commission, certification, 3662 
registration, employment, or volunteer service and shall not be further disseminated by the 3663 
participating agency. 3664 
 Sec. 3045. Fees. 3665 
 MPD may establish by rule a fee, to be imposed on a covered individual or participating 3666 
agency, for subscription of a covered individual in the District of Columbia Rap Back Program.  3667 
The fee may be imposed on an annual basis. 3668 
 Sec. 3046. Rules. 3669 
 MPD may issue rules, pursuant to Title I of the District of Columbia Administrative 3670 
Procedure Act, approved October 21, 1968 (82 Stat. 1204; D.C. Official Code § 2-501 et seq.), to 3671

164 
govern the implementation of the District of Columbia Rap Back Program and to otherwise 3672 
implement the provisions of this subtitle. 3673

SUBTITLE C. PUBLIC CHARTER SCHOOL EDUCATOR COMPENSATION 3721 
PAYMENTS 3722 
 Sec. 4021. Short title. 3723 
 This subtitle may be cited as the “Public Charter School Educator Compensation 3724 
Payments Act of 2025”. 3725 
 Sec. 4022. (a) In School Year 2025-2026, the Office of the State Superintendent of 3726 
Education (“OSSE”) shall make direct payments in a total amount of $30,785,832 from the 3727 
Workforce Investments account to public charter LEAs to increase the compensation of public 3728 
charter school educators.   3729 
 (b) To receive funds authorized under subsection (a) of this section, the public charter 3730 
LEA’s chief executive officer or head of school shall sign an assurance that the funds provided 3731 
under this section will be: 3732 
  (1) Used exclusively to increase the compensation of public charter school 3733 
educators; and 3734 
  (2) Paid to the public charter LEA’s educators during School Year 2025-2026. 3735 
 (c) For the purposes of this section, the term: 3736 
  (1) “Public charter LEA” means an individual public charter school or a group of 3737 
public charter schools operating under a single charter. 3738 
  (2) “Educator” means a school-based employee who directly supports students, 3739 
including full-time and part-time teachers, teachers’ aides, homebound teachers, hospital-based 3740

`MEMORANDUM 
 
TO:    The Honorable Phil Mendelson 
   Chairman, Council of the District of Columbia 
 
FROM:    Glen Lee 
   Chief Financial Officer 
 
DATE:    May 27, 2025 
 
SUBJECT:  Fiscal Impact Statement – “Fiscal Year 2026 Budget Support Act of 
2025” 
 
REFERENCE:  Draft bill as provided to the Office of Revenue Analysis on May 23, 2025 
 
 
Conclusion 
Funds are sufficient in the proposed fiscal year 2026 through fiscal year 2029 budget and financial 
plan to implement the Fiscal Year 2026 Budget Support Act of 2025.  
  
The District’s proposed fiscal year 2026 budget includes $11.9 billion in Local fund spending 
supported by $11.9 billion of Local resources, with an operating margin of $0.5 million. The estimated 
expenditures for the proposed General Fund budget, which includes Dedicated Taxes and Special 
Purpose Revenue in addition to Local funds, are $13.5 billion.   
  
The proposed budget and financial plan accounts for the expenditure and revenue implications of the 
bill.  
  
The bill, the “Fiscal Year 2026 Budget Support Act of 2025,” is the legislative vehicle for adopting 
statutory changes needed to implement the District’s proposed budget and financial plan for the fiscal 
years 2026 through 2029.  The following pages summarize the purpose and the impact of each subtitle.

TITLE I – GOVERNMENT DIRECTION AND SUPPORT 
 
Subtitle (I)(A) - Limitations on Liability Against the District of Columbia Act of 2025 
Background 
The subtitle places limits on the District’s unliquidated damages liability to persons or property 
resulting from actions by the District government. The subtitle limits damages to $500,000 for all 
claimants related to a single incident or occurrence. The limit is $1 million if the damages were 
awarded due to intentional wrongful acts or omissions by the District. The subtitle requires any 
defendant to expend more than $10,000 in medical expenses to be eligible for an award for 
noneconomic damages, except in cases of permanent disfigurement or death emanating from an 
intentional wrongful act. These limits apply to any case filed in court or another relevant tribunal on 
or after October 1, 2025.  
The collateral source rule prevents a defendant from reducing their liability in a judgment based 
upon the plaintiff having received compensation for the same incident from another source, such as 
an insurance company or worker’s compensation. The subtitle limits the application of the collateral 
source rule when damages are imposed on the District or its instrumentalities for any medical, dental, 
custodial, or rehabilitation costs related to personal injury, survivorship, or wrongful death. In 
related court cases, a plaintiff can only present evidence for the court’s consideration of actual 
amounts paid or required to be paid by or on behalf of the plaintiff.  
When an official District vehicle is on an emergency run at the direction of an authorized person, it is 
moving expeditiously on a mission or to a location to deal with an emergency or violation of the law, 
or to transport a sick or injured person. Incidents have occurred where the operator of the official 
vehicle on the emergency run injures a bystander or other party and that individual brings a claim 
against the District. District code does not define gross negligence to provide guidance to juries and 
judges when assessing fault and damages for these incidents.  
 
The subtitle establishes a definition of gross negligence related to an injury or other incident arising 
from an emergency run. The subtitle defines gross negligence as wanton, willful, and reckless 
disregard or conscious indifference for the rights and safety of others that results from an extreme 
deviation from the ordinary standard of care. Further, the subtitle prohibits a party from recovery 
arising from an emergency run if the party seeking recovery was contributorily negligent, including 
their failure to yield to a vehicle on an emergency run with its lights and sirens active.  
Financial Plan Impact 
In fiscal year 2024 the District exceeded its settlements and judgments budget, which is used to pay 
liability claims, by nearly $10 million. In fiscal year 2025, through January, the District has already 
exceeded its annual budget by $10 million. The subtitle’s three main provisions will limit the 
District’s potential exposure to claims. While each claim against the District is unique, establishing 
payment limits, ensuring that a plaintiff can only admit evidence of actual amounts paid or required 
to be paid (as opposed to cost estimates), and explicitly defining gross negligence will limit the 
District’s overall potential claims exposure. As a result, amounts budgeted for settlements and

Subtitle (I)(B) - Community Affairs Amendment Act of 2025 
Background 
The subtitle centralizes within the Executive Office of the Mayor (EOM) the grantmaking functions of 
the community affairs offices. The grant functions being centralized under EOM include grants made 
by the Mayor’s Office and Commission on African Affairs, the Mayor’s Office on African American 
Affairs, the Mayor’s Office on Asian and Pacific Islander Affairs, the Mayor’s Office on Caribbean 
Community Affairs, the Mayor’s Office on Latino Affairs, the Mayor’s Office of Lesbian, Gay, Bisexual, 
Transgender and Questioning Affairs, the Mayor's Office on Returning Citizen Affairs, and the Mayor’s 
Office of  Veterans Affairs.  
The subtitle makes the Access to Jobs Grant Pilot Program permanent. The Access to Jobs Grant 
Program supports the employment of returning citizens by subsidizing employers to hire up to ten 
returning citizens for work of up to 32.5 hours per week. An employer can apply to hire anywhere 
from one to ten employees. 
Financial Plan Impact 
The fiscal year 2026 budget includes multiple grants that will be issued by EOM as it centralizes the 
grantmaking process for community affairs offices. This includes $274,000 in recurring funding for 
the Access to Jobs Grant program. 
 
 
 
Subtitle (I)(C) - Surplus Property Sales Fund Amendment Act of 2025 
Background 
The Chief Procurement Officer is authorized to dispose of any District surplus property through 
transfers within and among agencies, auctions, scrap sales, destruction, or donations to tax-exempt 
organizations or other jurisdictions. The Office of Contracting and Procurement (OCP) deposits 
surplus property sales proceeds and administrative fees collected through disposition agreements 
into the Surplus Property Sales Fund.1 OCP uses resources in the Fund to pay the administrative costs 
of maintaining and disposing of surplus property.  
 
 
1 Supply Management Amendment Act of 2015, effective October 22, 2015 (D.C. Law 21-36; D.C. Official Code 
§ 2-358.05

The Honorable Phil Mendelson 
Fiscal Impact Statement for the “Fiscal Year 2026 Budget Support Act of 2025,” Draft bill as provided to Office 
of Revenue Analysis, May 23, 2025 
 
Page 7 of 69 
 
The subtitle expands the Fund’s allowable uses to include OCP operational costs.  
 
Financial Plan Impact 
The Fund is expected to generate approximately $5.8 million in revenue in fiscal year 2026. The 
subtitle does not change the Fund’s projected revenues, but it expands the activities for which OCP 
can expend the Fund’s resources. The Fund is a lapsing fund. At the end of fiscal year 2024, nearly 
$3.4 million lapsed from the Fund to the Local Fund.  
 
 
 
Subtitle (I)(D) - Office of the Chief Technology Officer Amendment Act of 2025  
Background 
The Office of the Chief Technology Officer (OCTO) directs the District government’s information 
technology strategies and the implementation of technology services for District agencies. OCTO also 
leads programs to expand access to high-speed internet throughout the District.  
 
The subtitle expands OCTO’s statutory day-to-day responsibilities to include protecting the 
confidentiality, integrity, and availability of the District government’s information technology 
systems, assets, and related information. OCTO is authorized to protect these resources from 
cyberattacks, theft, damage, disruption, misdirection, and breaches and detect, mitigate, defend, 
remediate, and respond to these attacks. The subtitle also establishes a fourth primary organizational 
function of security services within OCTO. Under this function, OCTO will lead the District’s 
cybersecurity efforts and establish a risk management and compliance program in the District 
government.  
 
The subtitle clarifies that OCTO’s authorities extend to all District government agencies, including 
independent agencies, except for the existing exceptions for the Council and District of Columbia 
Auditor. The subtitle maintains an allowance for these entities to enter into agreements with OCTO 
as needed. The subtitle repeals exceptions for the Office of the Attorney General and the Office of the 
Ombudsperson for Children. 
 
Financial Plan Impact 
OCTO implements security measures for the District’s information technology systems, assets, and 
related information. There are no costs to making these efforts part of OCTO’s statutory functions.  
 
 
Subtitle (I)(E) – Telework Policy Amendment Act of 2025  
Background 
The subtitle requires the Mayor to establish a telework policy for all agencies, except the Council and 
agencies of the legislative branch, and prohibits District personnel authorities from establishing a 
different telework policy without authorization from the Mayor. The subtitle also requires every 
agency to submit a report to the Mayor with information on the use of telework by employees, and it 
authorizes the Mayor to audit agency implementation of the telework policy.  
 
The subtitle also prohibits personnel authorities from entering into a collective bargaining 
agreement that includes or requires a telework policy.

of Revenue Analysis, May 23, 2025

Financial Plan Impact 
The subtitle does not impact the budget and financial plan.  
 
 
 
Subtitle (I)(F) – District Employee Paid Parental, Family, and Medical Leave Amendment Act 
of 2025 
Background 
The subtitle changes eligibility requirements and available benefits for the paid parental, family and 
medical leave program for District government employees.  
 
First, the subtitle requires a District government employee to be employed by the District for at least 
180 continuous days to be eligible.  
 
Second, employees must provide written notice to their agency describing the need for the leave and 
provide a schedule of expected hours during which the employee intends to take the leave. The notice 
must be provided at least ten days in advance, if the leave is foreseeable. If the leave is due to an 
unforeseeable event, the employee must provide details of the leave either orally or in writing within 
48 hours after the emergency occurs. The subtitle allows for a good faith negotiation on dates for 
taking leave and allows an agency to deny use of paid leave if an employee does not agree to a 
reasonable request to revise the dates.  
 
Third, the subtitle removes grandparents and siblings2 from the list of defined family members for 
which family care leave may be taken.  
 
Fourth, the subtitle reduces the maximum leave that can be taken when caring for a defined family 
member for qualifying family leave events from eight3 weeks per year to two. 
 
Fifth, the subtitle requires probationary employees to enter into a continuation of service agreement 
to stay on as an employee for at least 12 weeks after the use of paid parental, family or medical leave. 
Probationary employees who voluntarily separate prior to the end of a continuation of service 
agreement will be considered indebted to the District government for salary paid during the leave 
period.  
 
Financial Plan Impact 
The subtitle may reduce employee leave-taking and therefore overtime expenses at agencies where 
overtime is used to cover absences of essential employees. However, many factors affect use of 
agency overtime, including vacancies and workload needs, and no overtime budget reductions have 
been estimated or incorporated into the proposed budget and financial plan at this time.
